# Bugs

Known bugs, recorded by any loop and fixed by the bugfix loop.
Each bug: symptom, how to reproduce, suspected cause if known. Move fixed bugs to Fixed.

## Open

### `npm run test:coverage` prints one of two readings for the same tree: 99.65% lines and 96.3% branches, or 99.90% and 96.7%, almost all of it `orchestrator.js` (87% vs 99% lines, 32% vs 62% branches). The higher reading counts code no process ran, so the coverage loop's "file with the most uncovered lines" and every before/after percentage it quotes depend on which reading a run happened to get (found by human-directed investigation 2026-09-30)

- **Symptom:** 12 passing runs of `npm run test:coverage` on unchanged 48213ce7 code (node v26.10.0) printed two different `all files` rows. 5 runs printed `99.65 | 96.28–96.29 | 97.82` and 7 printed `99.90 | 96.69–96.72 | 97.82`.
  - **The difference is `orchestrator.js`.** In the low reading it's `87.18 | 32.26 | 57.89`, uncovered `190-194 240-252 314-316 318-326 …`. In the high one it's `98.65 | 62.50 | 57.89`, uncovered only `526-529 588-591`.
  - **The low reading is the true one.** I kept the raw V8 dumps for one run of each kind, about 770 processes apiece. In neither did any process execute `orchestrator.js` lines 190–194 or 240–252, yet the high reading reports them covered. Merging the same dumps directly (docs/code-metrics/coverage.cjs counts a block as covered when some process ran it) gives an identical result for all 11 runs with dumps: 19,781 of 19,904 mapped lines, 95.45% of branches, 97.82% of functions. No line flips between runs, so what the tests execute doesn't vary. Only node's table does.
- **Why it matters:**
  - **Target choice:** the coverage role's find step (src/roles.ts) runs this report and picks "the file with the most uncovered lines". Whether that's `orchestrator.js` depends on the reading, and its gap is real: 74 of its code lines never run in the unit tier.
  - **Before/after claims:** loops quote node's `orchestrator` figures as evidence and as proof, for example 21554a5f (`62.55% line / 36% branch`), 0e37b0bf (`orchestrator.js 97.74% → 99.40% lines`) and 5c415f70. A before/after pair taken from different readings shows a gain or a loss that no test caused.
- **Reproduce:** in a worktree at a fixed commit, run `npm run test:coverage` four or five times and compare the `orchestrator.js` row; both readings show up within a few runs. To see what actually ran, prefix the command with `NODE_V8_COVERAGE=<dir>` (suiteEnv passes it through, and node copies its per-process dumps there at the end), then look for an `orchestrator.js` range covering JS lines 190–194 in any dump. There isn't one in either kind of run.
- **Cause (suspected, not confirmed):** node builds the table by merging the per-process V8 dumps: the test workers plus every CLI child the tests spawn. V8 leaves out a nested block range whose count equals its parent's, so the same block is listed in some processes' dumps and not in others. Node's merge (`lib/internal/test_runner/coverage.js`) combines ranges per function, and its result evidently depends on which dumps it sees in which order. A rough emulation of that merge over the low run's dumps gave 78.6% lines for `orchestrator.js`, not node's 87.18%, so the exact mechanism is unconfirmed. Node's line figure also counts comment and blank lines inside code that ran as covered, which is why it sits above the executable-line figure in both readings.
- **Expected:** the same tree gives the same coverage numbers. For example, `test-runner.js --coverage` could compute its table from the raw dumps with a merge that counts a block as covered if any process executed it, as docs/code-metrics/coverage.cjs does, which was identical across 11 runs. Failing that, the coverage role's prompt should warn that node's table can flip for `orchestrator.js`, and that a before/after comparison is only valid between readings from the same merge.

## Fixed

### The stack lander runs a full build check over stacks whose every change is doc-only: the single-change path skips a doc-only delta ahead of main, but landStack only skips a re-stack whose delta from its own last-checked tip is doc-only, so a docs-only change batched with others, or left alone as a bisect's last step, pays a suite run and can be rejected on its failure (found by human-directed investigation 2026-09-30, fixed 2026-09-30 by bugfix loop)

- **Symptom:** readme's change 8f39eb4d (7 lines of docs/how-it-works.md) was rejected at 2026-09-30 11:59 PDT on a scope-`batch` check that went red while the host slept. That red is the host-sleep build-check entry, e2d6139b. The same change landed alone would have run no check at all. The sequence on build a27e0766:
  1. improve's code change and readme's doc change drained together as one stack.
  2. The stack check made no verdict. It timed out late twice, at 11:33 and 11:47, was remapped to an unverified `failed`, and so counted as red.
  3. The bisect landed improve alone after that prefix's retry passed at 11:54:39, which seeded main's new tip green.
  4. The bisect then called `landStack([readme])`. It ran a full check on main's verified tip plus one doc file, the check went red, and attributeRedCheck rejected the change.

  The event log has 128 batch checks since 2026-09-19 whose stack can be resolved from the `merged`/`land_failed` events. 5 of them ran over stacks where every change was doc-only. Besides readme's, those were:
  - 09-23 03:34, director + steward: passed in 78 s.
  - 09-28 11:23, plan: passed in 22 s.
  - 09-30 06:10, director + steward: passed in 75 s.
  - 09-30 13:57, steward + telemetry: passed in 59 s.
- **Reproduce:** queue two vetted doc-only changes from different roles, for example two BUGS.md edits, so the landing slot drains them as one stack (landBatchMax ≥ 2). The feed logs a `build_check` with scope `batch` and the suite's full duration. Either change landed alone goes through landApprovedChange → verifyLanding and runs no check. In a lander test, a check stub that counts its runs is called once by landVetted for two exempt-only requests; it should be called zero times.
- **Cause:** landStack (src/landing-batch.ts) runs runScopedBuildCheck on every attempt, with one exception: a re-stack whose `docOnlyRestack` holds. That is `exemptTreeDelta(checkedTip, tip)`, where `checkedTip` is the last tip *that same call* checked, so it's null on the first attempt. It never asks whether the stack's delta ahead of main is exempt; main here is the tip assembleStack stacked on. verifyLanding (src/landing-merge.ts) does ask, and skips its in-lock check for a "doc-only delta ahead of main … cannot break the build". landVetted's bisect calls landStack fresh for each prefix, so even a size-1 doc-only remainder pays a full check. The bisect's `suspect` bookkeeping also treats a verdict-less stack outcome (`unverified`: a timeout or kill with no verdict about the tree) as a real red. That's how the suspicion moved onto the doc-only remainder here once the code prefix landed.
- **Expected:** landStack skips the check when the stacked tree differs from its assembly base only in review-exempt paths (isExemptDiff over `base..tip`). It then runs the same cross-checks verifyLanding's exempt arm runs, the backlog heading structure and the false-fix claim, and seeds no baseline, as the doc-only re-stack already doesn't. Separately, a stack check that came back `unverified` could retry the whole stack instead of bisecting, since no change was implicated.
- **Fixed 2026-09-30 by bugfix loop:** landStack now asks the exemption question the single-change path asks: the stack's own delta ahead of its assembly base (isExemptDiff over `base..tip`, where base is the tip assembleStack stacked on — assembleStack now returns that base alongside the captured shas). A doc-only stack, or a bisect's size-1 doc-only remainder, runs no check; it runs the cross-checks verifyLanding's exempt arm runs instead (backlogStructureReason, falseFixReason), and one that fails returns `conflict` — the one-at-a-time fallback then lands each change through verifyLanding's exempt arm, which blocks or rejects it with the proper handling — and a skipped check seeds no baseline, like that arm. Regression tests (test/lander-bisect.test.ts, both shown failing against the old landStack): a stack whose every change is doc-only lands with zero batch checks — the planted check fails anything it runs on, so a broken skip fails loudly through the bisect rather than quietly via a count — and a red stack's doc-only size-1 remainder lands without the third check the old code paid.
- **Validation gap:** no-repro — the bug surfaced from the event log, and no existing test assembled a doc-only stack or a doc-only bisect remainder (every batch fixture pins code changes), so nothing in the suite reproduced it until the two new scenarios were written; closest tag, since once written the repro is fully deterministic.

### A sleep-evidenced check outcome is still attributed downstream: the gate's attribution path, attributeRedCheck, and the red-main baseline each treat a slept run's failure as the change's own verdict (found by bugfix loop 2026-09-30 while fixing the measured-sleptMs entry now under ## Fixed, fixed 2026-09-30 by bugfix loop)

- **Symptom:** runScopedBuildCheck now measures sleptMs on every run, retries a sleep-spanned failure once, and records a retry that also slept as unverified at the merge scopes. The consumers were not taught the evidence: at the gate scope the slept-retry failure stays a plain "failed" outcome, so review.ts's attribution (mainTipVerdict → reject) still attributes it to the change and deletes its ref with reasons naming a test-failure headline; attributeRedCheck (a single landing at LANDING_CHECK_FAILURE_LIMIT, a single-change batch) rejects it as the change's failure; and main-baseline.ts's runBuildCheck outcome — which now carries sleptMs — still caches a sleep-spanned failure as an authoritative red, ending a tick main_red on sleep weather.
- **Reproduce (unit-level):** with the scripted sleep sampler of test/build-check.test.ts (every attempt spans a sleep, the check always exits 1), drive reviewAheadOfMain at the gate scope and a single-change batch: today both reject the change and delete the ref; the expected behavior keeps the commit and reports the sleep.
- **Expected:** an outcome whose run carries sleptMs above SLEEP_SPAN_TOLERANCE_MS (or unverified: true) is never attributed to the change: the gate keeps the ref and its pass-after-sleep wording names the sleep instead of a flaky test, attributeRedCheck records unverified without a strike, and the baseline records the run as unverified rather than a red main.
- **Fixed 2026-09-30 by bugfix loop:** the consumers were taught the evidence, through one shared predicate — build-check-events.ts's `unverifiedTreeOutcome` (the outcome's explicit `unverified` flag, or its run's measured sleptMs past SLEEP_SPAN_TOLERANCE_MS). review.ts's gate now returns `unverified` before consulting main: the commit and its pin stay for a re-land with no strike, the warning names the sleep instead of a test-failure headline, and a slept failure that passes the gate's re-run verifies the tree with a slept-phrase warning instead of the flaky label. landingCheckRed counts no strike and keeps the pin for an unverified red, and attributeRedCheck — the batch bisect's last step included — keeps the ref and records the sleep in lastError instead of rejecting. checkMainBaseline caches nothing for a slept failure (the SHA stays unset, so the next consult re-runs and a clean verdict settles it), mainRedGate warns-and-proceeds under the role naming the sleep, and mainTipVerdict's `why` says the check ran while the host slept. Regression tests: the gate keeps the commit on an all-slept pre-check and names the sleep on a pass-after-sleep (test/review-precheck.test.ts), attributeRedCheck keeps the ref without a strike for both unverified spellings (test/attribute-red-check.test.ts), the baseline never latches a slept red (test/main-baseline.test.ts), and mainRedGate proceeds unverified with the sleep in the warning (test/main-red.test.ts). The seams that made this drivable — a `sampleSleep` test seam on the review context and on checkMainBaseline — are the fake the entry's unit-level repro needed.
- **Validation gap:** no-fake — the scripted sleep sampler already reproduced the slept check itself, but the consumers under test had no way to receive synthetic sleep samples end-to-end (reviewAheadOfMain's gate path and checkMainBaseline took no sampler), so the attribution behavior could not be driven until those injection seams existed; the real slept-through landing that found the bug remains the only full-fleet confirmation.

### A provider "Request timed out." — backendKind `timeout` — fails a tick that has already spent hours of authoring with no retry, though both sibling spellings of in-flight provider slowness get the session-resuming retry: the transient-retry predicate admits only `backendKind === "stream-severed"` among backend kinds, so the digest's #1 loss cause of 2026-09-30 dies at the finish line with nothing but a floor warning (found by telemetry loop 2026-09-30 from the failure digest's top loss cause `4.7 h · $0.16 — 2 ticks: Request timed out. (bugfix, coverage)`, correlated with the same day's stream-severed retry commit 3573b04b, which extended the retry set to one backend kind and left `timeout` the only unretried in-flight slowness spelling, fixed 2026-09-30 by bugfix loop)

- **Symptom:** the 2026-09-30 digest's largest loss cause is `4.7 h · $0.16 — 2 ticks: Request timed out. (bugfix, coverage)` — two authoring runs that burned a combined 4.7 agent-hours and then failed on the provider's own request timeout. The response they got: one `provider backend failure (request timed out) — the transient retry does not cover this kind; the fleet-wide hold watches for a storm` warning (the 3× warning cluster `provider backend failure (request timed out)` shows the kind classification working), a fleet-hold contribution, and an `error` tick that discards every turn of the run's work. Compare the two sibling spellings of the same failure family — a provider too slow or overloaded to answer an in-flight request: pi's idle-stream timeout text ("predict stream timed out", flagged `transientServerTimeout`) is retried with the session resumed, and undici's bare "terminated" (kind `stream-severed`) was promoted to the retry set by today's commit 3573b04b. "Request timed out." — kind `timeout` — is now the only in-flight slowness spelling whose hours of work are thrown away on first failure, and it is the most expensive one.
- **Reproduce:** point the provider at a server that accepts the request and then stalls past its own request timeout — or stub the pi shim to emit `Request timed out.` as the run's error text after a realistic run — and call `runRolePi`. The result carries `transientBackend: true`, `backendKind: "timeout"`, and no retry: the floor warning fires (src/loop-pi.ts), `foldUsage` runs once, and the tick ends `error` with the full authoring budget spent.
- **Cause:** the transient-retry predicate in `runWithTransientRetry` (src/loop-pi.ts) admits a backend failure only when `pi.backendKind === "stream-severed"`; `backendKind` (src/pi-stream.ts) maps `/request timed out/i` to `"timeout"`, which the predicate excludes. The backend-kind floor's design comment ("a dead backend is not something an immediate re-run beats", BUGS.md 2026-09-29) is sound for connection-down, 5xx and model-load — those mean the backend is gone. A request timeout is different: the provider accepted the connection and failed to answer in time, the same overload shape as the idle predict-stream timeout that already earns a retry, and it strikes mid-run, after the tick has paid for its hours.
- **Expected:** admit backendKind `timeout` to the transient-retry set — one session-resuming retry (the same continuation 3573b04b gave `stream-severed`), taken after the minute-scale pause the hint-less 429 branch already waits, since an overloaded provider's immediate re-request lands in the same queue. Connection, 5xx and model-load kinds stay warn-only. That converts the day's #1 loss cause into the recovery its sibling spellings already get, at the cost of one bounded re-run per tick.
- **Fixed 2026-09-30 by bugfix loop:** the transient-retry predicate in runWithTransientRetry (src/loop-pi.ts) now admits `pi.backendKind === "timeout"` beside `stream-severed`: the failed attempt folds before the pause (so the fleet-wide hold still sees the storm), the retry waits the minute-scale refill pause RATE_LIMIT_NO_HINT_RETRY_S the hint-less 429 branch waits — an overloaded provider's immediate re-request lands in the same queue — then resumes the first attempt's session once. Its own warning wording ("provider request timed out after accepting the connection — retrying the pi run once") replaces the floor's does-not-cover warning for this kind; connection, 5xx and model-load kinds stay warn-only. Regression test: "a provider request timeout (Request timed out.) warns and earns the one retry after the refill pause" in test/loop-pi.test.ts.
- **Validation gap:** no-fake (closest tag) — the fake-pi shim existed, but no test stubbed a run ending on "Request timed out.", so nothing in the suite exercised the timeout kind's retry treatment and confirmation required the new scripted scenario (fail once, succeed on the resumed session, one 60s injected pause).

### A build check that spans a host sleep but finishes inside its deadline reads as a real red: runBuildCheck records no sleep evidence unless the harness's own deadline fired late, so a single-change batch or landing attributes the sleep-expired tests to the change and rejects it, the gate's flake re-run files the sleep as a "flaky" test, and a baseline ends a tick `main_red` (found by human-directed investigation 2026-09-30, fixed 2026-09-30 by bugfix loop)

- **Symptom:** at 2026-09-30 11:59 PDT, readme's change 8f39eb4d was rejected with `build check failed (npm run test): AssertionError [ERR_ASSERTION]: expected exit 0 on a ready repo:`. The change was 7 lines of docs/how-it-works.md and no code. Its scope-`batch` check ran from 11:54:39 to 11:59:14 (275 s, 7 of 2,499 tests failed). `pmset -g log` has the host in Maintenance Sleep from 11:54:43 to 11:58:23 and from 11:58:33 to 11:59:05, which is 252 s of those 275. improve's batch check had passed on the same main at 11:54:39 (2,498 pass). The rejection reasons went into readme's next tick as readme's own failure. The power log reaches back to 2026-09-27 03:56 and records 569 sleeps. Over that span, 10 of 1,146 build_check events finished `failed` while the host slept through most of the run:
  - **organize's baseline, 09-28 12:50:** it slept 187 of its 209 s, and the tick ended `main_red`.
  - **bugfix's gate, 09-28 13:03:** it slept 65 of its 89 s. The re-run passed, and the warning read `gate check failed then passed on retry — flaky: AssertionError …`. That points the digest (and telemetry and bugfix after it) at a test that isn't flaky.
  - **coverage's gate, 09-30 09:38:** it slept 59 of its 116 s. Its re-run failed without a sleep, so that rejection may be genuine.
  - **dry's gate, 09-28 09:20:** it slept 168 of its 188 s and was rejected. Its first run had already failed in 22 s without a sleep, so that rejection may be genuine too.
  - **Two baselines:** improve's at 09-28 10:11 slept 85 of 103 s, and the `harness` baseline at 09-30 13:38 slept 115 of 140 s. Neither left a follow-up event.
  - **The other three** (feature's landing 09-28 04:11, bugfix's batch 09-28 11:22, improve's batch 09-30 11:47) timed out with late deadlines, the already-fixed path (BUGS.md 2026-09-21, 2026-09-28).
- **Reproduce:**
  1. With the Mac on AC, start `npm test` in a worktree.
  2. About 20 s in, sleep the host for 90–200 s (`pmset sleepnow`, or close the lid).
  3. On wake, the run finishes red well inside 300 s, with the tests that were mid-wait failing. Coverage runs 9 and 10 on 2026-09-30 (docs/code-metrics.md) failed exactly this way: every failing test's duration equals one pmset sleep.

  At the harness level, point `check.command` at a stub that records `Date.now()`, exits 1 if more than 60 s of wall time passed, and is slept through as above. runScopedBuildCheck then returns `failed` with no `deadlineLateMs`.
- **Cause:** runBuildCheck (src/build-check.ts) maps every nonzero exit to `failed`, "a deterministic failure of the build itself", unless the output names a broken toolchain. The only sleep evidence it records is BuildCheckRun.deadlineLateMs, which is set only when the harness's own deadline timer fires late. A sleep can be shorter than the check's remaining deadline but longer than a test's own wall-clock waits. Those waits then expire at the next wake, the suite exits 1 inside the deadline, and the run carries no mark of the sleep. Each caller treats that as a verdict:
  - **A `batch` of one, or `landing` at LANDING_CHECK_FAILURE_LIMIT:** goes to attributeRedCheck (src/landing-core.ts). Main's cached verdict is green, so the change is `rejected` and its ref deleted.
  - **`gate`:** the one flake re-run (src/review.ts) absorbs a single sleep but reports it as a flaky test. Clamshell maintenance sleep recurs every few minutes; on 09-30 from 06:45 to 07:48 the host slept 579, 218, 904, 918 and 753 s between dark wakes of about 10 s. A re-run that also spans a sleep goes to attribution and rejects.
  - **Baseline (src/main-baseline.ts):** a sleep-caused red is provisional, but the tick still ends `main_red`. If a second worktree also sleeps through its confirmation run, the red becomes authoritative until main moves.
- **Expected:** a `failed` run that spanned a host sleep gets the treatment a late-deadline timeout already gets. That means one clean retry at every scope. A retry that also slept is recorded as unverified: it isn't attributed to the change or cached as a red baseline, and the gate's warning says the run spanned a sleep instead of naming a flaky test. This needs a measured `sleptMs` on every BuildCheckRun, not only on a late deadline:
  - **macOS:** the kernel's last wake time (`sysctl -n kern.waketime`) moving past `spawnedAt` means the host slept during the check. `pmset -g log` has the exact intervals.
  - **Linux:** `/proc/uptime` (CLOCK_BOOTTIME) advancing faster than a monotonic clock measures time suspended.
  - **Why not a monotonic clock on macOS:** libuv's clock there counts time asleep (see the BuildCheckRun docblock), so it can't tell.
- **Fixed 2026-09-30 by bugfix loop:** every BuildCheckRun now carries a measured `sleptMs` (new src/host-sleep.ts: macOS's kern.sleeptime→kern.waketime span for the newest in-window sleep — libuv's clock cannot be differenced there; Linux's /proc/uptime minus the monotonic clock), carried on the build_check event beside the deadline fields. runScopedBuildCheck retries a `failed` run whose sleptMs exceeds the new SLEEP_SPAN_TOLERANCE_MS (5 s, beside the existing deadline tolerance) once at every scope, and a retry that also slept is recorded as unverified at the merge scopes — the reject reason and warning say "ran while the host slept Ns mid-run" instead of naming a test failure. A clean retry's verdict stands exactly like a first-time pass, which also dissolves the gate's flake mislabel for the common single-sleep case: the internal retry runs before review.ts's flake re-run ever sees the outcome. Regression tests: test/host-sleep.test.ts (both measurement paths, synthetic samples) and two tests in test/build-check.test.ts (retry-then-pass at the gate; retry-also-slept → unverified at landing). The downstream consumers that still attribute an unverified outcome are recorded as the open entry above.
- **Validation gap:** no-fake — nothing could suspend the host inside a test, so the sleep evidence itself had to become fakeable: the fix's injected SleepSampler is the fake that did not exist, and the offline tests exercise the retry and unverified classification through it. A real slept-through run (the 2026-09-30 fleet log this entry was written from) remains the only end-to-end confirmation of the measurement itself.

### A breaker trip is announced twice in the event feed: the error-streak circuit breaker's own `role_streak_paused` is followed one poll later by a generic `role_paused` from the pause gates' edge-triggered bookkeeping, mislabeled as an operator action (found by bugfix loop 2026-09-30 latent-bug hunt over the same day's error-streak circuit breaker commit 16983baf, fixed 2026-09-30 by bugfix loop)

- **Symptom:** every auto-pause the breaker performs produces two feed events: `role_streak_paused` ("role docs paused — 10 ticks failed in a row; fix the cause and resume it") from the gate itself, then on the next ~2 s poll a `role_paused` ("role docs paused — it stops starting new ticks") from the pause gates. The second event's contract (src/events.ts) names an operator pause via `tumwater pause --role <id>`, so the fleet-state-changes sections of the digest and the dashboards list one pause twice and attribute the second copy to the operator.
- **Cause:** in src/gate-polls.ts's `pollFleetGates`, `pollPauseGates` runs first each poll and advances its edge-triggered bookkeeping (`prevPausedRoles`) against the marker as it stood; `pollStreakGate` then writes the marker through `pauseRole`. On the next poll the marker's new member reads as a fresh operator pause and the pause gates log `role_paused` — the breaker pause is indistinguishable from one the operator typed, purely because of poll ordering.
- **Fix:** fold the trips into the pause gates' bookkeeping in the same poll: `pollFleetGates` adds each just-tripped role to `states.pause.prevPausedRoles` right after `pollStreakGate` returns, so the next poll sees no marker change and logs nothing; a later operator resume still logs `role_resumed` as usual. The wiring (gate-polls) is the home for the fold, not pause-gates or streak-gate — neither of those owns the other's state.
- **Reproduce (unit-level):** test/gate-polls.test.ts builds a LoopRunner at `consecutiveErrors` 10, calls `pollFleetGates` twice over the same gate state, and asserts exactly one `role_streak_paused` and zero `role_paused` events — before the fix the second poll emitted the duplicate (test failed `1 !== 0`).
- **Validation gap:** none — the closest fit: no existing test had driven the two pollers through the shared marker in order, but the offline two-poll unit repro (real pause marker, real events log, no fake or shim needed) reproduced and confirmed it directly.

### A model stream the provider severs mid-run — pi's bare "terminated" error — fails the tick with no transient flag, no retry, no per-run warning, and no fleet-hold contribution: the backend-kind floor keys on TRANSIENT_BACKEND, whose alternation (connection error, request timed out, 5xx texts, model-load) does not contain "terminated" — the exact spelling undici emits when it cuts the HTTP stream (the 2026-08-22 idle-timeout diagnosis, commit 4d6bc503) — so the digest's #2 loss cause of 2026-09-30 died in complete silence (found by telemetry loop 2026-09-30 from the failure digest's error cluster `1× telemetry · terminated`, correlated with the same day's 13:25 fleet hold where the sibling spelling "request timed out" got the full response, fixed 2026-09-30 by bugfix loop)

- **Symptom:** the 2026-09-30 digest's second-largest loss cause is `1.4 h · $0.02 — 1 tick: terminated (telemetry)` — a tick that ran 1.4 hours and then failed with the bare error text `terminated`. It appears in the top error clusters (`1× telemetry · terminated`) but in NO warning cluster: the backend-kind floor (BUGS.md 2026-09-29, part 1/2 — "the per-run floor now warns on every failed backend-kind run") never fired, the transient retry never ran, and the fleet-wide hold never counted it. Compare the same day's `request timed out` failures, which warned (`provider backend failure (request timed out) …`), contributed to the 13:25 fleet hold, and were answered end to end — same failure family (the model server severing an in-flight request), opposite response, purely because of which words the error text used.
- **Cause:** `src/pi.ts`'s `close` handler classifies only one stderr pattern (`TRANSIENT_PI_CRASH`, the JSON-parse crash) and otherwise passes `parser.errorMessage` (or the stderr tail) through verbatim as the run's error; `src/pi-stream.ts`'s `feedLine` sets `transientServerTimeout`/`transientRateLimit`/`transientBackend` only when the error text matches their regexes, and "terminated" matches none of them (pi-stream.ts:59-98). `runWithTransientRetry` (src/loop-pi.ts) retries only `transientServerTimeout || transientPiCrash || transientRateLimit` and warns only under `transientBackend` — so a severed stream falls through every layer: no `--continue` retry even though the session on disk is intact and a fresh attempt succeeds (the same transient-of-the-world property the floor's fix documented), no per-run warning, no hold contribution, and the tick's work is discarded as an ordinary error.
- **Fix sketch:** classify the stream-severed spelling as a backend failure — add `terminated` to `TRANSIENT_BACKEND`/`backendKind` in src/pi-stream.ts (a distinct kind such as `stream-severed`, since undici's idle-timeout cut and a provider-side termination both surface this bare word), and if the text can arrive only on the child's stderr rather than in a pi event errorMessage, classify stderr alongside `TRANSIENT_PI_CRASH` in src/pi.ts's close handler so the existing retry and hold machinery sees it. Keep the pattern anchored (whole-word or word-boundary) so a tool output or commit message that happens to contain "terminated" in an unrelated error text cannot fake a backend failure.
- **Reproduce (unit-level):** feed `PiStreamParser` a line whose event carries `errorMessage: "terminated"` (and separately, a run whose stderr ends in `terminated` with a nonzero exit) and assert the distilled `PiRunResult` — today every transient flag is false and the run classifies as a plain error; after the fix `transientBackend` is true with the new kind, `runWithTransientRetry` warns and retries, and the storm test can group a spread of them into a hold.
- **Validation gap:** real-run-needed — the closest tag: the failure only ever surfaced on a real provider severing a 1.4-hour live HTTP stream, and which transient layer (or none) a severed stream falls through could only be confirmed from that run's telemetry — the unit-level repro (feeding the parser the bare word, the fake pi ending on it) became possible only once the digest had named the exact spelling.

### A forced-restart press outlives the pending restart it targeted: a main revert that makes the build fresh exits the redeployer's poll before the cooldown check, leaving the one-shot forced flag armed, and the next stale head's poll waives a cooldown the operator never pressed to waive (found by bugfix loop 2026-09-30 latent-bug hunt over the same day's forced-restart feature commit 5c0f6884, fixed 2026-09-30 by bugfix loop)

- **Symptom:** the operator presses the dashboard's build-stale alert's refresh icon while a restart is pending inside its cooldown; before the next poll lands, main reverts to the built sha (or any other early return — a latched blocked head, auto-restart off) takes the poll out at `!stale || !autoRestart || blockedHead === mainHead`, which sits before the forced flag's only consumer. The flag stays armed indefinitely. When main later moves again — hours into the same cooldown, a stale head the operator has not seen — the poll reaches the cooldown check, consumes the stale flag, nulls `lastAutoRestartAt`, and starts an unattended hold+drain+swap episode mid-cooldown. "One press buys one waiver" instead buys a waiver for a different pending restart than the one on screen when the press landed.
- **Reproduce:** with the scripted deps, drive a completed restart (cooldown starts at `swappedAt`), poll a stale head inside the cooldown (`none`), call `forceRestart()`, then poll the built sha with staleness flipped fresh (`none`, early return), then poll a new stale head at `swappedAt + 2 h`: it returned `hold` and logged `restart_pending` — the evaporated press waived the deferral. After the fix it returns `none`.
- **Cause:** the flag's documented lifetime — "the next poll that reaches the cooldown check" — was defined by where it is consumed, not by what it targets. The poll path has four early returns ahead of that consumer, and none of them mentioned the flag; the one that marks the targeted restart's death (the build going fresh, which clears `staleSince`) cleared everything but the flag.
- **Fixed 2026-09-30 by bugfix loop:** the `!stale` branch of the poll's staleness update — the exact spot that ends a stale episode — now also clears `forcedRestart`, so the armed press dies with the pending restart it was pressed into. A blocked head keeps its flag (a blocked restart is still pending; the waiver applies when the block clears), and the ordinary forced path is untouched. Regression test: test/redeploy.test.ts drives press → fresh revert → re-stale inside the cooldown and pins that the later head defers with no episode started.
- **Validation gap:** unclear-invariant — the forced-restart tests landed with the feature pinned the happy path and the one-shot property, so nothing exercised a press whose targeted restart evaporated; confirming the bug first required reconstructing what the flag's one-shot scope was supposed to bound (the pending restart, not the calendar until the next stale poll), which the consume-side comment stated incorrectly.

### A prompt attachment whose client name's safe characters all strip away — a non-ASCII `截图.png` — is saved as an extension-less file the dequeue cleanup cannot match: savePromptImages re-derived the extension from the sanitized basename, whose extname is "", so the image cannot render as an image and orphans in the queue dir forever (found by bugfix loop 2026-09-30 latent-bug hunt over the same day's image-attachments feature commit 693ce725, fixed 2026-09-30 by bugfix loop)

- **Symptom:** an image dropped or pasted into the GUI composer under a name like `截图.png` — any name whose only [A-Za-z0-9._-] characters are its dot-extension — is written beside the queue file as `<stem>` with NO extension: pi's read tool keys off the extension, so the receiving loop's agent cannot view the attachment as an image, and `removeSameStemSiblings` (the dequeue and cancel cleanup) skips it — the entry's next character after the stem is neither "." nor "-" — so the image file outlives its prompt in the queue directory, one orphan per such attachment.
- **Reproduce:** `savePromptImages(root, role, queueFile, [{ name: "截图.png", dataBase64 }])` — the scratch repro (dist build of 693ce725) printed the saved name as `1727700000000-000001-1234` with no extension, and the file survived the dequeue-side stem-sibling cleanup; after the fix it is `1727700000000-000001-1234.png` and the cleanup removes it.
- **Cause:** savePromptImages derived the saved file's extension from `path.extname(safeImageName(image.name))`, but safeImageName strips every non-[A-Za-z0-9._-] character from the basename — `截图.png` sanitizes to the bare `.png`, whose path.extname is "" (a leading-dot name has no extension), so the stem got no extension appended. promptImagesProblem had already validated the RAW name's extname; the correct source was there all along.
- **Fixed 2026-09-30 by bugfix loop:** savePromptImages takes the extension from the validated raw name (`path.extname(image.name).toLowerCase()` — promptImagesProblem already guarantees it is one of PROMPT_IMAGE_EXTENSIONS) and safeImageName is deleted, so the client's basename is never used at all. Every saved image is now `<stem>.<image ext>`, exactly the shape removeSameStemSiblings removes with the prompt. Regression test: test/inbox-attachments.test.ts saves a `截图.png` and a `Bildschirmfoto.jpeg`, pins both extension'd names, and dequeues the prompt to assert the queue directory ends empty.
- **Validation gap:** unclear-invariant (closest tag) — the suite's attachment fixtures all used plain ASCII client names, so nothing exercised the sanitize-then-extname round trip; confirming the bug first required reconstructing what the sanitization was supposed to guarantee, since a "safe basename" and an "extension-preserving one" are not the same invariant, and the code's own comment claimed sanitization as if it were.

### `history --since` re-opens the just-fixed "Queued to land" bug for a tick that spans the cutoff: the ts-filtered row set keeps a spanning tick's tick_end and its landing outcome but cuts the land_queued pin the join bridges on, so a landed change reads "Queued to land" forever in the window-shaped view (found by bugfix loop 2026-09-30 latent-bug hunt over the same day's queued-row join commit 3f8b0f0f, fixed 2026-09-30 by bugfix loop)

- **Symptom:** `tumwater history --since <duration>` (the only readTickRowsSince consumer) shows a tick's row as "Queued to land" indefinitely even though the fleet table, the feed, and the plain `history` tail count all show the change landed — whenever the tick SPANS the since-cutoff: its `land_queued` pin and tick_start sit before the cutoff (logged during the tick), its `tick_end` and `landed`/`land_failed` outcome after it. The tail-count view is immune (a suffix window that holds a tick_end holds everything after it, and the join needs no older evidence there but the pin, which sits in the tick's own block), so the same landing resolves in one view and not the other.
- **Reproduce:** seed a log where tick 1 starts and enqueues (land_queued sha1) before the cutoff and ends `queued` after it, with `landed sha1` later; tick 2 queues and lands fully inside the window. `readTickRowsSince(root, since, null)` returns tick 2 `changed` and tick 1 `queued` — the scratch repro (dist build of 3f8b0f0f) printed exactly that before the fix.
- **Cause:** readTickRowsSince hands tickRows only the cutoff-filtered events, and resolveQueuedResult's pin-claim rule (newest unclaimed land_queued at or before the row's ts) finds no pin for the spanning row — its own pin was filtered out — so the row keeps the raw `queued` label even though its landing outcome sits right there in the row set. The prior fix's contract comment reasoned only about the OUTCOME side ("the landed/land_failed event sits after its tick_end, so any tail window holding the row holds the verdict too") and missed that the PIN side sits BEFORE the tick_end and does not survive a ts filter that keeps the row.
- **Fixed 2026-09-30 by bugfix loop:** the since scan reads its join evidence WIDER than its rows — readTickRowsSince makes a second readEventsSince call reaching TICK_ROW_JOIN_BACKSLACK_MS (6 h, longer than any timeout-bounded tick) behind the cutoff and hands it to tickRows as a TickRowJoin; the pin/outcome maps build over that superset (role-scoped like the rows), so a spanning tick's pin is present and the claim rule picks it. Soundness guard: a row whose tick_start sits BEFORE the join read's floor keeps the raw label — below the floor the day-keyed over-read makes pin presence non-monotonic, so claiming could steal an older tick's pin; a MISSING start needs no guard (rotation drops a prefix, so pins older than a lost start are lost with it, and a skipped tick has no start but is never queued). The tail-count path passes no join and behaves byte-identically. Regression tests: test/history-data.test.ts pins the spanning-pin resolution end-to-end through readTickRowsSince (both ticks resolve, each to its own change) and the floor guard on the pure tickRows (start before the floor keeps raw; floor below the start resolves).
- **Validation gap:** unclear-invariant — the existing suite could not catch it because every queued-join fixture put the pin inside the window; confirming the bug first required reconstructing the invariant that BOTH halves of the join evidence (the pin before the tick_end, not just the outcome after it) must survive whatever filter keeps the row, which the prior fix's contract comment had stated for the outcome half only.

### A queued tick's row reads "Queued to land" forever, even after its change has landed: the GUI's History view and the loop drawer's recent-ticks list render the tick_end event's transient `queued` result as final and never reconcile it with the landed/land_failed event that resolves it (found by director 2026-09-30 from a user report — "sometimes in the UI, it says 'Queued to land' when I think the actual status is already Landed", fixed 2026-09-30 by bugfix loop)

- **Symptom:** in the GUI, a tick that produced a change shows "Queued to land" (indigo) in the History view's result column and in its loop drawer's recent-ticks list — indefinitely. The change may have landed minutes later (the activity feed shows the merge, the fleet table's last result reads "Landed" green, the commit is on main), but the tick row keeps the pre-landing label. The user reading history cannot tell whether the change actually landed.
- **Reproduce:** run the fleet until any loop lands a change, then open the GUI's History view (or that loop's drawer): the tick row whose work was landed still reads "Queued to land", while the fleet table and the feed show it landed.
- **Cause:** the tick rows (src/history-data.ts's `TickRow`, served to the GUI and printed raw by `tumwater history`) are built purely from `tick_end` events, and a queued tick's `tick_end` carries `result: "queued"` (src/tick-finalize.ts) — a transient pipeline state, not a completed outcome (src/tick-outcome.ts: a queued tick is deliberately never stored as `lastResult`). The GUI's RESULTS map (src/ui/gui-client-model.ts, `queued: ["Queued to land", "indigo"]`) renders it like any final verdict, and nothing joined it with the later `landed`/`land_failed` event for the same change. The join the log already supported: `land_queued` (logged at enqueue, during the tick) and `landed`/`land_failed` both carry the change's `commit` sha, while `tick_end` itself carries none.
- **Fixed 2026-09-30 by bugfix loop:** the collector resolves it — `tickRows` (src/history-data.ts, the one home shared by `tumwater history`, `history --json`, and the GUI's /api/history, so the History view and the loop drawer's recent-ticks list both pick it up) now indexes each loop's `land_queued` pins and `landed`/`land_failed` outcomes, and each queued `tick_end` claims the newest `land_queued` at or before its ts that no newer row has claimed (the one logged during that tick), then resolves to the first same-loop, same-sha landing outcome after that pin: "changed" renders as "Landed" green, a `land_failed` renders its own failure result ("Merge conflict", "Rejected in review", …). A landing genuinely still in the pipeline — or one whose outcome event matches no pinned sha — keeps the raw "Queued to land" label, the conservative fallback; no window growth was needed, since a tail-count read that holds a `tick_end` always holds the events after it too (documented in readTickRows' contract comment). Regression tests: test/history-data.test.ts pins the queued→landed and queued→land_failed joins tick-by-tick, the still-pending raw fallback, the different-sha non-join, and the join's survival under the `--role` filter and across interleaved loops.
- **Validation gap:** unclear-invariant — the suite stayed green because the collector's `result` was a plain event passthrough; confirming the bug first required reconstructing the invariant that a row's result names the tick's FINAL outcome, with `queued` a transient pipeline state that the later landed/land_failed event resolves — nothing could fail until that join existed and was pinned.

### The GUI sidebar's "Main green · 2429/2430" drops the skipped count, so a fully green suite with one skipped test reads like one failure; the TUI/status header badge for the same check says `2429/2430 (1 skipped)` (found by human-directed investigation 2026-09-30, fixed 2026-09-30 by bugfix loop)

- **Symptom:** with main's newest check at `{tests: 2430, pass: 2429, fail: 0, skipped: 1}`, the GUI sidebar row reads "Main green · 2429/2430". An operator reading it next to a red "main is red" banner (see the entry above) took the missing test for a failure. The skip is visible only in the row's hover tooltip.
- **Reproduce:** open the GUI Fleet page on any fleet whose latest merge-scope check has `skipped > 0` and compare the sidebar "Main …" row with `tumwater status`'s header badge.
- **Cause:** src/ui/gui-client-fleet.ts built its own counts string (`" · " + c.counts.pass + "/" + c.counts.tests`) instead of reusing the wording of `mainCheckBadge` (src/ui/badges.ts), which appends `(N skipped)` when `skipped > 0`. That's the drift between the two observer surfaces that badges.ts exists to prevent.
- **Fixed 2026-09-30 by bugfix loop:** the counts fragment is phrased once in badges.ts's new `mainCountsFragment` — `pass/tests` with a parenthetical naming every non-passing count, `(2 failed · 1 skipped)`, so a failure reads explicitly too — and ships preformatted as the payload's new `mainCounts` field (status-payload.ts, same omit-re-derivation idiom as `mainCheckBadge`); the sidebar row renders it verbatim. `mainCheckBadge` now builds its counts from the same helper, so the TUI header picks up the explicit-failed wording too. Regression tests: test/badges.test.ts pins `mainCountsFragment`'s wording (skip named, fail named, zero counts bare, no counts empty) and `mainCheckBadge`; test/status.test.ts pins `mainCounts` in the payload; test/gui-client-sidebar.test.ts executes the sidebar's newly marked `// sidebar:` region under the clientScope stub harness and asserts the rendered row carries the skip and fail counts.
- **Validation gap:** no-fake — the sidebar's browser script is one served blob with no offline execution path (its region was unmarked and undriven, so only a live GUI page showed the drift); the fix marked the region and ran it under the existing clientScope stub harness, which is what now confirms it — the closest tag, since the shim itself existed but nothing had wired this surface to it.

### A loop's `main red` phase outlives main's recovery: the dashboard raises "main is red — 1 loop is blocked" while the sidebar right under it reads "Main green", because the phase comes from the loop's own last tick result and nothing compares it with main's newer green check (found by human-directed investigation 2026-09-30, fixed 2026-09-30 by bugfix loop)


- **Symptom:** on 2026-09-30 the GUI Fleet page showed the red banner "main is red — 1 loop is blocked / Code loops stop authoring until main's suite passes again" and, at the same moment, the sidebar row "Main green · 2429/2430". Timeline (UTC, events.jsonl): 12:33:53 `organize`'s baseline check on `11b93d11` failed (the `kill() alone tears down the whole run tree` flake, fixed by `fc9af8d5` in the same push) and its tick 404 ended `main_red`; 12:35:07 main moved (`wake: main moved`); 12:35:27 `clean`'s baseline passed, 12:38:30 `bugfix`'s passed; `organize`'s `nextRunAt` (12:35:53) came and went while it waited for a slot, so its `lastResult` stayed `main_red` until tick 405 started at 12:40:35. For ~5 minutes the banner told the operator main was red and a loop was blocked, and neither was true; the loop's row showed "Main red" too. The window has no upper bound: it lasts until that loop next starts a tick, which a long sleep, a full `maxConcurrent`, a pause, or a spent budget can push out by hours.
- **Reproduce:** have a loop end a tick `main_red`, then let main turn green (another loop's baseline or a landing's merge-scope check passes) before that loop ticks again. `tumwater status`/TUI/GUI keep that loop in phase `main red`, and the GUI raises the `mainred` alert, while `mainCheck` (the sidebar row, the header's `· main <sha>: green` badge) says green.
- **Cause:** `loopPhase` (src/ui/status-model.ts:223) returns `"main red"` whenever `s.lastResult === "main_red"` (and no queued summary), with no check against the fleet's current view of main. `fleetAlerts` (src/ui/fleet-alerts.ts:90) counts loops in that phase to build the banner, so it inherits the staleness. The comment above line 223 calls this "self-correcting, since each blocked tick re-records main_red while red and a green wake overwrites lastResult". That covers the next tick but not the gap before it. Meanwhile the snapshot already carries the fresher verdict: `mainCheck` (src/status-data.ts, newest merge-scope `build_check`, with `sha` and `at`).
- **Fix:** `loopPhase` (src/ui/status-model.ts) takes the snapshot's `mainCheck` as an optional last parameter — passed by `loopRowCells`, the single-homed row derivation both dashboards share — and retires the `main red` label when `mainCheck` passed strictly after the loop's `lastTickEndedAt` (new helper `mainCheckRecovered`): the fleet-level green is fresher evidence than the loop's own red, so the label falls through to the ordinary sleep/queue state and the `mainred` banner (fleetAlerts, which reads the rendered phase) follows automatically. A newer failed or skipped check, a check older than the red tick, or an unknown tick end all keep the label; no `mainCheck` at all keeps the old behavior for direct callers. The `sha` comparison is unnecessary: a passed check stamped after the red tick means main's suite passes on main's current state whatever the sha. Regression tests in test/status-model.test.ts pin the phase through both `loopPhase` and `loopRowCells`, and that `fleetAlerts` raises no `mainred` banner for the recovered loop while still raising one for a genuinely stale blockage.
- **Validation gap:** real-run-needed — the staleness window only exists between a loop's ticks on a live fleet (sleep, a full slot pool, a pause, or a spent budget can hold it for hours), so confirming it took a real run's dashboard-plus-events timeline; no offline check compared a loop's phase with `mainCheck` until this regression test pinned the state combination.

### The failure digest's cluster examples are hard-sliced at 120 chars mid-word with no ellipsis or remainder marker, so the tail that names the repro — the failing assertion's file, the recovery action, the rejected claim — is silently invisible to the operator and to every loop that reads the digest (found by telemetry loop 2026-09-30, fixed 2026-09-30 by bugfix loop)

- **Symptom:** the 2026-09-30 digest cuts four cluster keys mid-word with nothing marking the cut: the day's top error cluster ends `Expected values to be strictly equal:) — authoring skipped u` (the slice eats `until main is green`, leaving the reader unable to tell whether authoring was skipped or urged), the red-main warning ends `code mer`, the clean-role rejection ends `the commit claims "comme`, and the bugfix rejection ends `an empty \`PI_CODING_AGENT_DIR\` di`. A bugfix loop sent at a rejection it can only read as `…PI_CODING_AGENT_DIR di` cannot reproduce it; the digest is the fleet's primary evidence channel and it hid the distinguishing tail of its own most expensive cluster.
- **Cause:** `src/failure-cluster.ts` sliced `message.trim().slice(0, EXAMPLE_MAX)` for every cluster example and `src/failure-report.ts` rendered `${c.example}` raw; the same bare slice cut the loss-cause examples in `src/failure-data.ts` and the landed-commit summaries (SUMMARY_MAX). The byte bound itself is deliberate (`src/failure-report.ts` documents "every free string is capped"); the defect was that the cut was a bare `String.slice` — no ellipsis, no word boundary, no pointer — while the digest's own cluster-count overflow right next to it prints a proper remainder marker (`+2 more clusters holding 2 warnings`).
- **Fix (2026-09-30, bugfix loop):** `truncateExample(message, max)` in src/failure-cluster.ts — a message within budget passes through untouched; a longer one is cut at the last whitespace inside the budget (hard cut only when no boundary exists) and always ends `… (+N chars)` naming exactly what was dropped, so a truncated example can never read as a complete message. Applied to cluster examples (clusterMessages), the digest's loss-cause examples, and the landed-commit summaries (SUMMARY_MAX); render sites stay raw because the marker rides in the example. Cluster KEYS keep their bare slice — grouping wants byte-stable prefixes, not marked display text — and the report's byte-bound docblock now records the marker as part of the cap. Regression tests in test/failure-cluster.test.ts pin the through-untouched, word-boundary, no-boundary, custom-cap, and clusterMessages-marking behaviors.
- **Validation gap:** no-observability — the silent cut left no trace anywhere: every example was a plain string that passed every existing test and rendered as a complete message, so nothing in the suite or the digest itself flagged the defect; confirming the fix meant first writing the missing assertion that a cut must carry its `… (+N chars)` marker.

### `spawnCli().kill()`'s 3 s backstop SIGKILLs the supervisor mid-graceful-stop when full-suite load slows the stop past the grace, so the "kill() alone tears down the whole run tree" regression test fails with exit code null instead of 0 and main's suite goes red (found by the red-main gate 2026-09-30, fixed 2026-09-30 by bugfix loop)

- **Symptom:** `npm run test` on main at 1a251c4e failed exactly one test: test/cli-run-live.test.ts's `kill() alone tears down the whole run tree` asserted `expected a clean supervisor exit` with `actual: null` (death by signal) after the full graceful-stop output (announce, tick aborted, orchestrator stopped) had already printed. The same test passed green when run in isolation.
- **Cause:** test/cli-harness.ts's `KILL_GRACE_MS` was 3 s — the SIGTERM→group-SIGKILL backstop behind `kill()`. Under the loaded 2430-test suite the same graceful stop that takes ~1 s alone (its own SIGTERM test: 1.5 s total) ran past 3 s, and the backstop fired while the supervisor was still shutting down: `process.kill(-pid, "SIGKILL")` hit the supervisor itself (it leads the group the shim spawned it into), so `exitCode()` saw a signal death instead of the exit-0 `process.exit(code)` was about to perform. The same class of flaw was already fixed in the product once — process-group.ts's escalation "armed at spawn SIGKILLed every healthy check longer than KILL_GRACE_MS" — the shim just kept the too-short constant.
- **Fix (2026-09-30, bugfix loop):** `KILL_GRACE_MS` in test/cli-harness.ts raised from 3 s to 10 s, matching the SIGTERM→SIGKILL escalation grace the product itself uses (KILL_GRACE_MS in src/process-group.ts), so a slow-but-progressing stop is never murdered; and the backstop callback now re-checks `child.exitCode`/`signalCode` before sweeping, so a close that raced the timer cannot convert a finished clean exit into a signal death. The backstop's purpose (a wedged stop cannot hang a test file) is unchanged. The existing regression test is the regression test — it failed red under load and its assertion (exit 0, stdout EOF) is the invariant; no new test added.
- **Validation gap:** no-repro — the failure never reproduced deterministically (the isolated test passed every time); only the full loaded suite reproduced it, so confirming the fix meant re-running the whole 2430-test suite under the same load rather than a targeted check.

### A real supervisor that dies without forwarding its signal — SIGKILL from the OOM killer or `kill -9`, or an uncaught crash — orphans the whole orchestrator generation at PPID 1, running unattended with its stdio tied to a dead terminal, and nothing in the harness notices (found by bugfix loop 2026-09-30 while fixing the spawnCli().kill() half of the same shape, fixed 2026-09-30 by bugfix loop)

- **Symptom/cause:** `supervise()` (src/supervisor.ts) forwards only SIGTERM to the generation, and the generation (src/cli-run.ts, SUPERVISED_ENV) had no parent-death watch: SIGKILL to the supervisor pid cannot be trapped or forwarded, so the generation reparents to launchd and keeps ticking the fleet until something kills it — the same leak shape test/cli-harness.ts's `kill()` had before its 2026-09-30 fix (sibling entry below), but in production, where no test finally stands in to sweep. The end-of-run `TUMWATER_RUN` sweep never runs for a killed supervisor either.
- **Fix (2026-09-30, bugfix loop):** the supervised generation now starts a parent-death watch — `startParentDeathWatch(stop)` (src/supervisor.ts, wired in cmdRun after the signal handlers): a poll of the parent pid every 5 s (PARENT_POLL_MS) that fires the same graceful `stop()` a forwarded SIGTERM would have run the moment the pid changes, i.e. the generation has been reparented by a supervisor that died without stopping it; the timer is unref'd so it never holds the event loop open. Watching for any ppid change (not `=== 1`) also covers hosts with a subreaper. Regression tests in test/supervisor.test.ts: an injected-ppid unit test (fires exactly once on a pid change, never on an unchanged one, self-clears) and a real-process test — middle process SIGKILLed after its grandchild (which imports the compiled watch) reports started, the grandchild's watch fires and it exits. Scratch repro before the fix: middle+grandchild probe, `kill -9` the middle, grandchild alive 1.2 s later (orphan); after the fix, same probe against the built watch: grandchild runs its stop path and exits.
- **Validation gap:** no-fake — confirming the fix needed a real SIGKILL-then-reparent process tree (a killed supervisor's grandchild), a fixture the suite did not have; the new real-process probe (temp-dir middle + grandchild importing the compiled watch) supplies it, so the confirmation stays offline with no model or live fleet involved.

### `spawnCli().kill()` (test/cli-harness.ts) SIGKILLs only the supervisor, so any `tumwater run` e2e test that fails before its own SIGTERM step leaks a live orchestrator generation to PPID 1 — which holds the test's stdout/stderr open, so the test file never exits and a `node … | tail` tool call hangs the tick indefinitely (found by human-directed investigation 2026-09-30, fixed 2026-09-30 by bugfix loop)

- **Symptom:** on 2026-09-30 at 04:51 PDT, dry tick 455 ran `node dist/test/cli-run-live.test.js 2>&1 | tail -12`. The command never returned, and the dashboard raised "dry looks stuck — no pi output for 5m27s". `ps` showed the test process (73936) alive at 0% CPU with no children. Next to it was `node …/worktrees/dry/dist/src/cli.js run` (80640, PPID 1, cwd a `$TMPDIR/tumwater-test-run-*/tumwater-test-*` fixture repo). That orphan was still ticking a fake pi across all 14 roles. Its fds 1/2 were the test's stdout/stderr pipes, so neither the test's `child.stdout` reader nor `tail` ever saw EOF. `kill 80640` unblocked the tick.
- **Trigger:** dry's uncommitted refactor of test/cli-run-live.test.ts dropped the `writeConfig` call from "run starts the fleet, prints its banner, and stops cleanly on SIGTERM". The fleet started with every role, `assert.match(s.out(), /loops: clean/)` failed before the test sent SIGTERM, and the `finally` ran `s.kill()`. The trigger was a test bug. The leak and the hang are the harness's fault, and any early assertion failure in these tests would reproduce them.
- **Fix (2026-09-30, bugfix loop):** `spawnCli` now spawns detached, so the child leads its own process group, and `kill()` sends SIGTERM first — the supervisor forwards it and the fleet takes the real graceful stop path; `gui`/`logs -f` die on the default disposition — then, after a 3 s grace (KILL_GRACE_MS), SIGKILLs the whole group (`process.kill(-child.pid, "SIGKILL")`), which reaches the generation and anything it started even when the graceful stop wedges or the supervisor is already gone; a child that already exited gets the group sweep immediately. The regression test `kill() alone tears down the whole run tree` (test/cli-run-live.test.ts) drives kill() as the sole stop — the exact finally-after-a-failed-assertion shape — and asserts the supervisor exits 0 and stdout reaches EOF: EOF is the observable, because the generation holds an inherited copy of the pipe's write end, so the read end closes only when the generation itself dies. Scratch repro before the fix (mimicking the old spawnCli): stdout EOF within 6 s of SIGKILL: false, one orphaned generation at PPID 1; after the fix (driving the real spawnCli): EOF true, exit 0, no orphans.
- **Validation gap:** no-observability — a green suite can never trigger it (the leak fires only when a test is already failing mid-run) and the symptom was a hung tool call with no failure trace inside the suite, so nothing in `npm run test` could confirm or refute it; confirmation came from a bounded scratch reproduction plus the new EOF-based regression test.

### The doctor CLI tests in test/doctor.test.ts assert exit 0 against the host's REAL process table, so any orphan anywhere on the machine fails `npm run test` and the review gate rejects every landing until someone kills the leak by hand (found by organize loop 2026-09-30 while re-landing fd4a19be, fixed 2026-09-30 by bugfix loop)

- **Symptom:** on 2026-09-30 03:48 the review of fd4a19be (a pure import-wiring extraction that had passed the full suite in its own worktree minutes earlier) failed its build check at test/doctor.test.js's `doctor --json prints the collector's own payload` — `expected exit 0`, actual 1. Re-running the same suite on clean main reproduced it: doctor reported `fail orphans — pid 12703 … (run 5647-d3d485539d8b; harness 5647 exited)` — a test-victim process leaked by an earlier run's own test suite, hanging forever (`setInterval(() => {}, 1 << 30)`) with PPID 1, which `checkOrphans` counts by mark wherever its cwd is (the 2026-09-30 mark fix deliberately made cwd irrelevant). The organize loop killed the leak by hand; the suite then passed unchanged.
- **Why it is a bug:** the two doctor CLI wiring tests ("doctor exits 0 on a ready repo" and the --json one) run the real CLI, whose orphan check scans the host's process table with the system probe — the tests' comment "a fresh repo has no worktree, so no orphan either" holds for argv/cwd matching but not for byMark matching, which counts any PPID-1 carrier of a dead run's mark regardless of repo. A fleet that leaks one process (see the sweep entries in Fixed — leaks have happened repeatedly) makes every loop's build check fail until a human kills the leak, and the failure names an unrelated commit as its cause.
- **Fix:** the two CLI wiring tests now run under a hermetic PATH entry (`hermeticHostBins(psRan)`, test/doctor-fixtures.ts): a fake `pi` (exit 0) whose bin dir also holds a fake `ps` that touches a marker file and prints an empty process table, so the CLI's orphan scan — which resolves `ps` through PATH exactly like every other fake command the suite installs — sees no rows regardless of the host's real state. Each test then asserts the marker file exists, pinning the hermeticity itself: a future edit that lets the CLI probe fall through to the host's table fails loudly in the suite instead of silently at the next real leak. No product code changed — doctor correctly reporting a real orphan is right, and stays covered by the unit seams in test/doctor-orphans.test.ts; only the wiring tests' view of the host became deterministic. Reproduction before the fix: a staged orphan (backgrounded `node -e 'setInterval(...)'` carrying `TUMWATER_RUN=999999-repro1`, PPID 1) failed `doctor exits 0 on a ready repo` with the exact byMark fail line; the same tests passed with the orphan still alive after the fix.
- **Validation gap:** no-fake — the suite could not confirm the bug until an orphan actually existed on the host, and no seam handed the CLI a fake process table (the unit tests stub `ProcessProbe` directly, the child-process CLI tests could not); confirming the fix took a staged real orphan process plus the new fake-`ps`-on-PATH fixture that seam now supplies.

### The 12 h restart cooldown defers redeploying onto newer main even while the RUNNING build's own commit carries a red test verdict, and the deferral had no urgency carve-out: a red running build is the exact predicament the self-redeploy exists to end, yet the episode (hold → drain → swap) waited out the full 12 h like ordinary churn (part 2/2 of the 2026-09-30 entry; part 1/2, the prewarm, is Fixed below) (found by telemetry loop 2026-09-30, fixed 2026-09-30 by bugfix loop, re-landed same day after a review objection to the cold-cache recovery story)

- **Fix:** the deferral now has an urgency carve-out: when the RUNNING build's own commit carries a settled red baseline verdict, the cooldown deadline is cut from 12 h to 15 min (RESTART_URGENT_COOLDOWN_MS, src/redeploy.ts) — still a real delay so consecutive reds cannot storm, but the fleet no longer knowingly executes failing code for half a day. The verdict comes from the running build's own SHA, not the deferred head, and is re-consulted on every poll: a red arriving mid-cooldown shortens the remaining wait immediately, and the deferral warning is now keyed to the deadline rather than a once-per-episode flag, so the onset warns once more with the earlier deadline (and `status()`/the dashboards publish the earlier deadline). The cold-cache path — flagged in review on the first attempt, whose read-only cache peek could never fire because checkMainBaseline only ever baselines a main-tip worktree, never a stale running SHA — is now real: with no cached verdict, the redeployer runs the baseline check once on the build SHA itself in a dedicated detached witness worktree (.tumwater/worktrees/_build, src/paths.ts), tracked in the background so poll() still never awaits a suite run. That run costs at most one suite run per process per build SHA, lands in the same fleet-shared cache, and is priced into the feed as a baseline build_check event. Safety both ways: a false red only brings forward a restart the episode still gates on the new head's green check, compile, and boot gate; a false green (or skip/error) leaves the ordinary 12 h cooldown untouched.
- **Validation gap:** no-repro (closest fit) — the bug surfaced in live-fleet telemetry and every existing cooldown test pinned the unconditional 12 h deferral as correct, so nothing in the suite failed; confirmation came from the carve-out regression tests written alongside (urgent window fires, onset warns once more with the earlier deadline, unknown/unsettled verdict never triggers it), and the cold-cache recovery path needed a fixture repo whose declared check runs offline — the unit seam had modeled the verdict as a read-only peek, which review showed could never fire.

### The 12 h restart cooldown idled away the deferred head's verification: poll() returned at the cooldown check before any episode work began, so a head whose restart the cooldown deferred sat unverified for up to 12 h and then paid green-check + compile + drain from zero when the deadline lapsed — the 2026-09-30 shape had the fleet on build 29356ddd while main waited one commit ahead at 5ab4e072, recovery postponed to 15:03 local with zero verification work done in the meantime (part 1/2 of the 2026-09-30 entry; its urgency carve-out half is Fixed above) (found by telemetry loop 2026-09-30, fixed 2026-09-30)

- **Fix:** during an active cooldown with a stale build and autoRestart on, poll() now prewarms the deferred head (src/redeploy.ts `prewarm()`) with the same side-effect-free effects the episode itself runs, once per SHA: the per-head green check (deps.mainGreen — its baseline check events keep the wait observable on the dashboards), then, only after a green verdict, the staged compile into that head's staging dir. When the cooldown lapses, the episode adopts the prewarmed trackers (`adoptPrewarmedGreen`/`adoptPrewarmedCompile`): a finished verdict (green or red; compile ok) is reused so no check or compile runs twice, a check or compile still running at the lapse is adopted rather than started a second time against the same mirror worktree and staging dir, and a prewarm that finished WITHOUT a verdict — a rejected check or a rejected compile, the "could not run" shapes — is never adopted, so the episode re-runs it fresh under its own rejection rules (BUGS.md 2026-09-16) and a repaired environment cannot be wedged replaying a dead tracker. A red prewarm verdict feeds the existing block path unchanged; a finished FAILED compiler verdict is also recompiled rather than adopted, so every block decision rests on a verdict the episode's own step produced. The rate limit itself is untouched: the fleet still ticks on the stale build through the whole cooldown, and no hold or drain begins early.
- **Validation gap:** no-repro (closest fit) — the bug surfaced in live-fleet telemetry and the suite's cooldown tests pinned the blind window as correct ("no green check for the deferred head"), so no existing case failed; confirmation came from the prewarm/adoption regression tests written alongside the fix, which the old assertions had to be rewritten to make room for — and the FAILED-verdict adoption shape (a prewarm compile finishing `{ok:false}` during cooldown) was itself caught only by re-review, no test initially covering it; it now has its own regression test.

### doctor's orphan check cannot see a marked cross-group leak whose run's harness has exited (part 2/2 of the 2026-09-30 sweep fix): `checkOrphans` still counts a PPID-1 process only when its argv or cwd lies under `.tumwater/worktrees/`, while every process a run now starts carries `TUMWATER_RUN=<harness pid>-<nonce>` in its environment — the mark names both the run and the harness whose exit makes the carrier an orphan, wherever its cwd is (found by bugfix loop 2026-09-30 while fixing the sweep half of this entry), fixed 2026-09-30

- **Symptom:** the sweep fix (2026-09-30) reaps marked leaks at run exit, but a leak that survives anyway (a SIGTERM-trapping process, a sweep whose table scan failed, a SIGKILLed harness that never reached its sweep) is invisible to `tumwater doctor` when it sits outside the worktrees: `checkOrphans` matches argv/cwd under `.tumwater/worktrees/` only, and the prompts send agents to exactly the scratch dirs (`/tmp/twprobe`, `/tmp/tumwater-cov-dist`, `$TMPDIR/tumwater-test-run-*`) where such leaks live.
- **How to reproduce:** spawn a detached node process with `TUMWATER_RUN=999999999-deadbeef` (a dead harness's pid) in its environment from a scratch dir, then run `tumwater doctor`: the orphan check reports ok.
Fixed 2026-09-30: `checkOrphans` (src/doctor-orphans.ts) now reads each parentless process's environment through a new `ProcessProbe.runMarkers` — the same scan `sweepRunMarker` runs (`/proc/<pid>/environ` on Linux; one `ps -wwE -A -o pid=,command=` pass on macOS, which hides platform binaries' environments, so a sleep/sh leak stays unseen there, as the sweep's own scan does) — and counts any PPID-1 process whose EVERY `TUMWATER_RUN` mark names a dead harness pid (`pidAlive`), wherever its argv or cwd points; a mark naming any live harness is left alone, because that harness's run sweep kills everything carrying the mark when its run folds. A mark without a `<pid>-` prefix judges nothing, and a recycled pid reading as live (the merge-lock check's limit) can leave such a straggler unseen. An itemized mark-caught orphan names its run and the dead harness's pid, and the check's count line now reads "orphaned processes" — a mark-caught leak need not be a worktree process.
**Validation gap:** no-fake — the fake process table had no environment channel, so the suite could not even express a marked leak outside the worktrees; confirming needed the probe fake extended with run-marker reading (plus one live-child smoke of the real reader), and no real run was required beyond that.
### The end-of-run process sweep (3ff19995) never reaches anything a tool call backgrounds: pi 0.87.1's bash tool starts every command `detached`, in its own process group, so `terminateChild`'s `kill(-pi.pid)` misses it and tool-call leaks outlive their ticks by hours (found by human-directed investigation 2026-09-30), fixed 2026-09-30

- **Symptom:** at 02:05 PDT on 2026-09-30, `ps` showed PPID-1 survivors of three ticks that had all ended normally:
  - `node -e 'require("http").createServer(()=>{}).listen(7199)'`, pgid 78527, still listening on *:7199. It came from improve tick 319 (20:16–20:17 on 09-29); the tool call at 20:17:02 was `cd /tmp/twprobe && node -e '…listen(7199)' & …; kill %1`.
  - Two `node --test` workers for `hang.test.js`/`hang2.test.js`, one listening on a random port. They came from improve tick 315 (19:14–19:17), whose probe SIGKILLed its `node --test` runner and orphaned the workers.
  - After coverage tick 410 ended at 02:11, a `node --test …/tumwater-cov-dist/test/loop-pi.test.js` worker was still parenting a real `pi` connected to oMLX, alongside four more real pis whose workers had already exited. runPi under test had detached each into its own group (the dangling-shim entry above).
- **Cause:** runPi's `'exit'` handler (src/pi.ts, "Sweep the group pi led however the run ended") signals pi's own process group. Its comment assumes a tool call's `(server &)` "stays in pi's group", which is false under pi 0.87.1. `createLocalBashOperations` → pi's dist/core/tools/bash.js spawns the shell with `detached: process.platform !== "win32"`. It calls `killProcessTree` only on abort or timeout, and after a normal completion it stops tracking the pid. So a backgrounded child lives on in the tool call's own group, which nothing ever signals. The 3ff19995 regression test models the tool call as a fake pi that backgrounds from its own shell, in the same group as pi, so it passes while the real agent leaks.
- **Also invisible to doctor:** `checkOrphans` (src/doctor-orphans.ts) counts a PPID-1 process only when its argv or cwd lies under `.tumwater/worktrees/`. Tool-call leaks from a scratch dir (`/tmp/twprobe`, `/tmp/tumwater-cov-dist`, `$TMPDIR/tumwater-test-run-*`) are never reported. The prompts send agents to exactly those dirs ("or from a scratch directory under the system temp"). (The doctor half is now its own Open entry, part 2/2 below.)
- **How to reproduce:** in a scratch repo, run a real `pi --print --mode json` whose only bash call is `(sleep 3600 &)`. After pi exits and runPi's sweep runs, `ps -o pid,ppid,pgid,command | grep 'sleep 3600'` shows the sleep alive, with PPID 1 and a pgid different from pi's.
- **Expected:** when a pi run ends, everything its tool calls started is gone, as 3ff19995 intended.
- **Fix direction:** stop relying on process groups for the sweep. runPi can put a per-run marker in pi's environment, e.g. `TUMWATER_RUN=<role>:<tick>:<pid>`. It should append to, not overwrite, any marker it inherits, so a nested harness under test keeps the outer mark. Every tool call and its descendants inherit the marker regardless of group or reparenting. At run exit, a same-user scan (`ps -E -ww -o pid=,command=` on macOS, `/proc/<pid>/environ` on Linux) finds and SIGTERMs the marked processes. doctor can then report PPID-1 processes carrying the marker of a run that is no longer live, wherever their cwd is. The regression test must drive the real shape: a fake pi whose "tool call" is a `detached` shell that backgrounds a child.
Fixed 2026-09-30: runPi stamps every run's environment with a per-run `TUMWATER_RUN` mark (`<harness pid>-<random hex>`, appended to any inherited mark so a nested harness under test keeps the outer run's) and, when pi exits alongside the group sweep, SIGTERMs every same-user process whose environment carries the mark — SIGKILL after 10 s — reading the table through `/proc/<pid>/environ` on Linux and one `ps -wwE -A -o pid=,command=` pass on macOS. That pass hides platform binaries' environments (sleep, sh), so a leak of those stays the group sweep's and doctor's problem; the leaks that matter — servers, test workers — are userland binaries ps -E does report. The regression test now drives the real shape: a fake pi whose tool call spawns its child detached, a group leader in its own right, so the group sweep cannot reach it.
**Validation gap:** no-fake — the suite already had 3ff19995's regression test, but its fake modeled the tool call as a same-group background job, so the suite stayed green while the real agent leaked; confirming this fix needed the fake that did not exist, a shim whose tool call spawns detached exactly as pi 0.87.1's bash tool does.

### The failure digest labels each cluster with its OLDEST verbatim message, so a cluster that outlived a config change shows the retired value: 23 900-second review timeouts from 09-28/29 render as "36× … 09-22 → 09-29 — timed out after 1800s" (found by human-directed investigation 2026-09-30), fixed 2026-09-30

- **Symptom:** on 2026-09-30 the operator read `tumwater report --failures` (and the dashboard digest) as the fleet "seeing lots of 1800s timeouts", but nothing had timed out at 1800 s since 2026-09-22 22:40. The review-failure cluster "**36×** bugfix, clean, coverage, dry +2 more · 09-22 → 09-29 — timed out after 1800s" holds 13 `timed out after 1800s` (09-22) and 23 `timed out after 900s while still making progress — …` (20 on 09-28, 3 on 09-29). The 900 s ones reflect the review timeout that applies today.
- **Cause:** `normalizeClusterKey` maps every duration to `<dur>` and `poolTimeoutKey` pools the progressing variant, both intentionally: one cause, one knob. But `clusterMessages` (src/failure-cluster.ts) takes `example` from the first message it sees, and events arrive oldest-first, so the rendered example is the oldest occurrence. The loss-cause fold in src/failure-data.ts does the same, setting `example` when it creates the draft. The `09-22 → 09-29` range is right; the value printed next to it comes from the wrong end of that range.
- **How to reproduce:** run `collectFailureReport` over a `review_failed` "timed out after 1800s" at t0 and three "timed out after 900s while still making progress — …" at later times. The cluster's example reads `timed out after 1800s`.
- **Expected:** the example shows the cause as it happens now: the newest verbatim message, the one at `lastSeen`.
- **How it was reproduced:** `clusterMessages` fed an 1800s timeout at t1 and 900s ones later returned the 1800s example; `collectFailureReport` over the same shape showed it in both `errors.clusters` and `lossCauses` — now a regression test in each (test/failure-cluster.test.ts, test/failure-report.test.ts).
- **Fix:** both `clusterMessages` and the loss fold (src/failure-cluster.ts, src/failure-data.ts) now replace `example` whenever a message arrives with `ts >= lastSeen`, keeping the single-example byte bound; the `Cluster.example` and `LossCause.example` docs now say "newest". One same-ts test assertion updated (write order wins at equal ts, display-equivalent among identical causes).
- **Validation gap:** unclear-invariant — the existing suite did not miss the behavior, it pinned the wrong one (a unit test asserted the first-occurrence example), so the example's intended contract had to be reconstructed before the bug could be confirmed as a bug.
